Named Defect Classes

Individual bugs are patched. Recurring bug classes are named — and every name is a standing instruction to build a detector. Naming a class is what turns G-INVARIANT-DETECTOR from an aspiration into an action: "this bug is an instance of <class>; the detector for <class> must now exist." That is the mechanism that stops whack-a-mole. A class whose detector does not yet exist is itself an open item under G-INVARIANT-DETECTOR.

G-INVARIANT-DETECTORGovernance ProcessRecurring Defect Classes Require a Detector

Accomplishment: Every named recurring defect class has an identified detector or an explicitly recorded pending detector; a class cannot be described as covered until the detector demonstrates failure against a counterexample.

Vision link: This process prevents known failure patterns from silently corrupting scope, confidence, price, or booking behavior.

P-GOVERN-DEFECTCLASSPrincipleWhen a bug recurs, it acquires a name.The class name and signature remain durable even when the implementation changes, so future instances can be recognized rather than re-diagnosed from scratch. The table states the required detector; whether that detector is implemented and passing must be established by executable evidence, not inferred from its presence in this Charter.

Vision outcome protected: turning recurring failures into recognizable defect classes with detectors, preventing repeated one-off patches from hiding a shared cause.

Rule health & evolvability

Vision outcome — turning recurring failures into recognizable defect classes with detectors, preventing repeated one-off patches from hiding a shared cause.

Harm prevented — A defect can survive because the check measures a convenient proxy, misses relevant cases, or shares the same faulty assumption as the implementation it is meant to verify. The specific commitment at risk is: turning recurring failures into recognizable defect classes with detectors, preventing repeated one-off patches from hiding a shared cause.

Implementation freedom — The implementation may change its representation, algorithms, component boundaries, wording, and interaction sequence. The enduring requirement is the outcome stated above—not today’s filename, function, framework, or arrangement.

Reconsider when — Reconsider when evidence shows that turning recurring failures into recognizable defect classes with detectors, preventing repeated one-off patches from hiding a shared cause fails under a counterexample that passes the claimed check or a changed architecture that makes the check observe the wrong boundary, or when a simpler responsibility boundary preserves the same outcome with fewer exceptions. Change the mechanism through governance; do not bypass it silently.

Evidence mechanism — Use representative customer paths and counterexamples that could falsify the claim. Verify that interpretation, evidence, decision, and customer-facing explanation remain consistent; a checklist or single happy path is insufficient.

Why a Named Class and Not Just a Battle

The Battle narratives record what happened and why, chronologically. A named class is the durable pattern those Battles are instances of. The two are complementary, not redundant: the Battle is the evidence, the class is the search key. When a new bug arrives, the first question is no longer "have I seen this before?" — it is "which named class is this an instance of?" Once the class is identified, the owning invariant is known, and the detector either fires (if it already exists) or is demanded (if it does not).

The classes below are lifted out of the Battle narratives deliberately. The Battles keep their evidence; the classes keep their names. The relationship is stated in each class row.

The Classes

ClassSignature — what it looks likeOwning invariantsRequired detector
DEFECT-ARBITRATION
Arbitration		A resolver returns a resolved value; a caller composes that return with a second source — a || chain, an ?? chain, a whitelist that selects between pre-computed candidates, or an inline re-derivation of the same concept. The "final" value is therefore not traceable to any single named source. A plausible number appears, but its provenance is a call-site decision rather than a resolver declaration.R-INVARIANT-PROVENANCE, R-INVARIANT-CANONICAL, R-INVARIANT-DUPLICATION-TICKET, P-INVARIANT-NOPATCHProvenance-field check. A resolver's return value carries an explicit source field. The detector fails when a call site composes a resolver's return with a second source, or when a resolved value reaching pricing cannot be traced to a named source on its own return value.
DEFECT-PERUNIT-SCOPE
Per-visit / per-unit scope		A one-time fee is multiplied by quantity (charged once per door), or a genuinely per-unit fee is added only once. The error is invisible at qty=1 and scales linearly with quantity.R-PRICE-SCOPE (Battle 4), R-PRICE-REACHESSchema-enforced scope field plus quantity-range sweep. Every modifier declares scope: "per_unit" | "per_visit"; the sweep asserts fee behavior across the full quantity range, not just the reported case.
DEFECT-DUPLICATE-PARSER
Duplicate parsers		Two independently-written parsers exist for the same input surface. The live preview and the priced result can structurally disagree. Common shapes: a private nested copy inside a renderer; three module-level functions declared twice with the later silently shadowing the earlier; a legacy path carrying its own object extractor.R-SYSTEM-ONEPARSE, R-INVARIANT-ONEUNDERSTANDING (Battle 15), R-INVARIANT-CANONICALSingle-parse structural test (fails if a second parser exists) plus duplicate-function detector (fails if any module-level declaration appears twice).
DEFECT-DERIVED-OVERWRITE
Derived overwrite		A compiler or regeneration step silently replaces hand-curated content it cannot itself reproduce. A routine re-run reclassifies a group the catalog deliberately classified; a curated field disappears; a derived namespace becomes the de-facto source because the raw field was clobbered.P-SYSTEM-NOEDIT, R-DOMAIN-COMPILER (Battle 3), R-INVARIANT-DERIVATION, P-INVARIANT-PROPAGATECompiler re-runnability test — regenerates into a scratch area and asserts the derived output is a superset of the raw source's curated fields. Hand-curated content must be preserved outright, not merged.
DEFECT-SECOND-CLASSIFICATION
Second classification copy		The routing-mode (or any other derived classification) exists in more than one place, and the copies drift. Consumers disagree about what the same group is because they are reading different copies of the same classification.P-SYSTEM-CLASSIFY-ONE, R-SYSTEM-PICKER-AGREEClassification-count test — asserts each group has exactly one routing-mode classification and that every consumer reads from that one source. Detector fires when a second copy appears.
DEFECT-DUPLICATE-REGISTRY
Duplicated registry / constant		Any list, constant, or registry used by more than one function is defined more than once. The copies start equal and diverge silently; a fix applied to one copy does not apply to the other.R-INVARIANT-SINGLEDEF, R-INVARIANT-DUPLICATION-TICKETDuplicate-function detector — asserts no module-level declaration appears twice and no shared constant has a second copy.
DEFECT-PATH-SPECIFIC-PATCH
Path-specific patch		A behavior is fixed on one entry path (or one gateway) but the equivalent behavior is not fixed on the others. The reported bug is closed; the same bug remains live on a path the reporter happened not to take.P-INVARIANT-NOPATCH, P-INVARIANT-VERIFY (Battle 1, Battle 7)Cross-path equivalence sweep — asserts that a fix applied to any one gateway produces the same result on every other gateway for the same facts. Detector fires when one path produces a different result from the others.
DEFECT-PREVIEW-CHARGE-MISMATCH
Preview-vs-charge mismatch		The sentence the customer watches compose and the sentence the price is built from are structurally different. Usually a downstream symptom of DEFECT-DUPLICATE-PARSER, but tracked separately because the customer-visible failure mode is distinct: a trust mechanism breaks.R-CLIENT-PREVIEW, R-CLIENT-CONVERGE, R-INVARIANT-ONEUNDERSTANDING (Battle 15)Preview/price agreement test — asserts the canonical intent rendered in the preview is the same canonical intent consumed by pricing, on every entry path, for every request shape.
DEFECT-AXIS-CONFLATION
Axis conflation		A tier, checkout state, or scheduling decision was made using a property that belongs to a different axis: units counted as skill, minutes counted as difficulty, tier used as a proxy for time, risk used as a proxy for complexity. The visible symptom is a job classified in a way that does not match the work it actually contains — twenty routine swaps and ten skilled installs sharing a tier because they share a duration.R-COMPLEX-AXES, R-COMPLEX-VOLUME, R-PRICE-DURATIONSTATE, R-PRICE-SCOPEAxis-independence test — fails if any resolved route's tier changed while the only input that changed was quantity; fails if a route over the duration threshold resolved to a flat-rate checkout state; fails if a service's complexity declaration is missing any required core dimension.
DEFECT-NON-RETIRING-ANSWER
Non-retiring answer		An intake module carries a fallback option — "not sure", "tech measures on site", "describe in notes" — that resolves to the same price, time, branch, and prep as a confident option, and carries no modifier. The fallback grants confidence points on the way in. The on-site visit supersedes the answer on the way out. The customer pays the friction cost of a question whose answer was never going to matter.R-INTAKE-NONRETIRING, R-INTAKE-WIREREMOVE, P-CONF-NOQUESTIONMATH, R-COMPLEX-AXESDelta check — enumerates every module in intake_modules, computes the (price, time, branch, prep, tier, risk classification, batch-risk flag) tuple each option produces, and fails when a fallback produces the same tuple as a confident option without carrying a modifier.
DEFECT-WITHIN-WORD-MATCH
Within-word match		A text matcher finds a term inside a longer word, or finds a multi-word phrase whose words are merely scattered through the sentence, and the SSOT term list is then read as if the customer had meant the term: "cement" inside "replacement" gives a brick wall to every "… Replacement" service, "art" inside "apartment" prices a mirror hang differently from the same sentence without the word, "lock" inside "blocks", "new" inside "renewed". Its mirror image is the same defect seen from the other side: a term the customer would write that cannot be found by its own name, because the matcher or the vocabulary was written for a different spelling. The class returns because one site is fixed while its sibling matchers keep the substring rule; nothing crashes and the price moves by a few dollars or minutes.R-INVARIANT-CANONICAL, R-INVARIANT-ONEUNDERSTANDING, P-INVARIANT-VERIFYExplained-hit check — the behavior-focused tag-detection check. Drives the real engine over the catalog's own phrases and fails when a tag is found that no whole-word or whole-phrase reading of its own terms explains; fails when a tag cannot be found by its own name; fails when appending a clause that says nothing about the job ("in my apartment") changes the service, the tags or the price; each leg is shown to flag a planted substring matcher.
DEFECT-DANGLING-REFERENCE
Dangling reference		A hand-authored pointer in the SSOT resolves to nothing, or is spelled in a way one of its readers does not recognise, so the thing it was meant to do silently does not happen: a retired tag still suggested by a dynamic service; a then key typed with an ASCII hyphen against an option label typed with a non-breaking hyphen, so the follow-up question never opens; option tags in three spellings of which the one reader that compares them reads one. Nothing crashes, the compiler's validator can report the file valid, and the catalog looks complete. The pointer is wrong for as long as nobody takes the branch that needs it.R-SYSTEM-SHAPE, P-INVARIANT-PROPAGATE, R-INVARIANT-DERIVATIONReference-resolution check — the SSOT reference-integrity check. Walks every place the raw SSOT can hold a pointer (tag pointers, chain and then modules, then keys, tag answers, modifier, formula and checkout-state references, scoping and membership) and fails on one that resolves to nothing or is not in the one spelling its reader reads; shows that the compiler's validator reports each class on a copy broken in exactly that way; each walk is shown to find a planted defect.
DEFECT-STATE-IGNORANCE
State ignorance		The system holds a fact and asks the customer for it anyway, so the customer supplies it again. One class, three named shapes, told apart by where the ignored fact came from. DEFECT-REASKED-QUESTION: the fact is in the request's own state, an answer already given or a tag in force because the customer typed it, tapped it or the system set it; a customer who types "mount a TV on brick wall" is asked what kind of wall, and a symptom tile the customer tapped is asked again as a symptom question. DEFECT-REDUNDANT-TRAVERSAL: the customer walked a path that declared the fact, a group they chose or a tile they tapped, and the intake asks for it anyway; Brick or Concrete > Mirror asks what is being mounted with the mirror as one of five answers, and Drywall or Plaster > TV Flatscreen asks the wall type with "Brick or concrete" among the answers. The BookingContext keeps only the last group and none of the tiles, which is how the path's facts are lost before the question is built. DEFECT-UNPRUNED-FLOW: the fact is structural to the job itself, such as the group the service lives in or its default tags, so in this branch an answer could not be true, and the question is presented as if it could. The question is well formed and the price is right; what is spent is the customer's time and their sense of being heard. It is the mirror of DEFECT-NON-RETIRING-ANSWER: that class is a question whose answers move nothing when nothing is known, this one is a question the system could already answer. The governing distinction is durable: a redundant question is one the system could already answer from available state. R-INTAKE-WIREREMOVE covers answers inferable from free text (REASKED-QUESTION), navigation (REDUNDANT-TRAVERSAL), or structural knowledge (UNPRUNED-FLOW). These are distinct grounds for removal under that Rule: a question that was never needed, and an answer that moves nothing.P-INTAKE-INFER (free-text, navigation-path, redundancy and structural inferability), P-CLIENT-NAVFACT, R-INTAKE-WIREREMOVE, R-INVARIANT-ONEUNDERSTANDINGKnown-fact check — the state-ignorance check. Drives the real page: walks every group, picker level and tile of the catalog and types every service's name and every tag phrase that answers one of its questions, and judges each question the customer is shown, by one function, against what is known (answers given, tags in force, the groups on the path, the service's own group, a tile that changed nothing). Every instance is filed in the ledger and held in a baseline that can only shrink; a new one fails. It reads each card one answer deep as well as when it opens, because answering one question can reveal the next. It finds the instances the operator named by the mechanism, not by a list, and finds instances in groups nobody named; each shape, and the one-answer-deep read, is shown to fail on a planted copy of the page or catalog. The sweeps that remove the instances are separate items.
DEFECT-TWO-PURPOSESAn artifact is asked to serve two roles at once: SSOT as both authored knowledge and machine-optimized index; compiler as both derivation and validation; test as both behavioral proof and source-shape pin; deployment file as code-organization model. Neither role can evolve independently.R-SYSTEM-ONEPURPOSEMaintain a registry of named artifacts and declared roles; flag an artifact whose consumers or checks require an undeclared second role.
DEFECT-STALE-SANDBOXA test extracts functions by name from a hand-maintained list; the list drifts from the deployed source and the test measures a shape that no longer exists.R-TEST-CANFAILthe stale-baseline check — it must fail when the extracted baseline is stale.
DEFECT-SILENT-SKIPA check is guarded by a condition with no failing else branch, or a test reports SKIPPED and exits successfully when its required environment is absent. The check vanishes instead of failing.R-TEST-CANFAILthe NLP harness boot check and the verification runner skip semantics; verify missing prerequisites produce an explicit non-zero failure unless the skip is an intentionally declared non-gating observation.
DEFECT-TEST-CANTFAILA test cannot fail for the defect it claims to detect: for example, it reads a hidden node, compares two equally broken builds, or exercises a condition that cannot occur.R-TEST-CANFAILMutation detector: introduce a controlled mutation representing the claimed defect and require the test to fail; passing under that mutation disqualifies the test as a detector.
DEFECT-FROZEN-INTERFACEA field observed by a consumer becomes an accidental contract; changing its shape breaks the consumer despite no deliberate interface decision.R-INVARIANT-FROZENCompare published schema/interface versions and require an explicit compatibility decision for every breaking field-shape change.
DEFECT-DERIVED-AUTHORITYA derived artifact becomes the de facto specification because consumers read authored knowledge that has no source in the SSOT; for example, curated parent, IDs, or picker vocabulary exists only in a compiled index.R-SYSTEM-ONEPURPOSEProvenance detector: every derived field names its authored source; any curated value authored only in the derived artifact is flagged.
DEFECT-STALE-DOCUMENTA living document describes an earlier system shape, points to a removed path, or lists a defect class absent from the class table.R-DOC-NOSTALE, R-DOC-SINGLEPURPOSEDocumentation-integrity check: resolve every referenced path and reconcile every named defect class against the class table.
DEFECT-UNVERIFIED-CLAIMA comment or note claims that something was confirmed, verified, or tested without identifying the check or evidence that supports the claim.R-SYSTEM-UNVERIFIABLE-CLAIMScan: flag verification language in comments without a test name, command, or source location that substantiates it.
DEFECT-NAMES-AS-KEYSA relationship or lookup depends on a display label, title, spelling, or punctuation rather than a stable identifier; renaming text silently breaks the relationship.R-SYSTEM-IDENTITYthe SSOT reference-integrity check — extend the existing reference check to flag label-keyed relationships and options.
DEFECT-CROSS-CONCERN-DRIFT
Cross-concern drift		The same shared mechanics are implemented independently at multiple call sites and the copies diverge, such as quantity handling, suggested-tag parsing, defaults merging, or confidence arithmetic. The defect is competing implementation, even when each copy appears locally reasonable.R-SYSTEM-ONECONCERN, R-SYSTEM-CONCERN-HOMEMap each shared concern to its canonical implementation; flag competing mechanics and report call sites whose output diverges under the same inputs.
DEFECT-CONCERN-ABSORPTION
Concern absorption		A module takes ownership of an adjacent concern because it is convenient to the current call path, even when that module is not the concern’s proper home. The concern then becomes coupled to that module or spreads as shadow logic. Example: pricing owns confidence arithmetic because pricing needs the confidence result.R-SYSTEM-CONCERN-HOMECompare each cross-cutting concern’s declared owner with the module performing its decisions/calculation; flag responsibility mismatches even when no duplicate implementation exists. The detector must include a counterfactual fixture in which moving the computation into the wrong module causes failure.
DEFECT-GOD-FUNCTIONOne function performs multiple unrelated responsibilities, so each layer can pass its tests while the function remains difficult to reason about and change safely.R-SYSTEM-UNFUNCTIONCohesion check: measure function size and branching complexity, apply a declared threshold, and require an explicit bounded exemption for any retained outlier.

How a Class Becomes a Detector

The path from a new named class to an appropriate detector is the same every time:

  1. Name the class. The first time a bug is recognized as a recurrence of an existing pattern, or the first time a genuinely new pattern is recognized as likely to recur, a row is added to the table above. The name is stable; the description may sharpen.
  2. Identify the owning invariants. Which of the Charter's existing rules does this class violate? A class with no owning invariant is either a rule the Charter is missing (and the Charter must be amended) or a bug that is not actually a class (and should be filed as a one-off instance).
  3. Specify the detector. What mechanism, run on every test, distinguishes an instance of this class from a passing system? The detector's shape follows from the class's signature. If the detector can be a source-shape test and the class is genuinely about source shape, so be it; if the class is behavioral, the detector must drive the real interface (see P-GOVERN-GOODHART).
  4. Ship the detector. A class whose detector does not yet exist is an open item. A detector belongs in CI when the owning verification policy requires continuous protection; CI membership is implementation coverage, not what makes the class a Rule.
The load-bearing claim.The reason whack-a-mole happens is that bug instances get patched while bug classes go unnamed. Naming a class converts the next instance from a diagnosis into a lookup. The arbitration class — the one this revision adds — is the clearest case in the project's history: the defect persisted across years of governance precisely because no name existed for the shape of the bug, so every instance was argued about on its own terms rather than recognized as the same failure mode returning.

The Arbitration Class, Specifically

Arbitration is the class this section was primarily added to name, and its relationship to the rest of the Charter is worth stating once and clearly:

  • The defect: caller-side composition of a resolver's return with a second source. The resolver correctly chooses; the caller then re-chooses.
  • Why it survived so long: each individual call site looked like a reasonable safety net — a fallback for when the resolver returned nothing, a whitelist to constrain the resolver's output, an inline re-derivation to "verify" it. No single site looked like a bug. The shape only becomes visible when named.
  • Why R-INVARIANT-PROVENANCE fixes it: once the resolver's return value carries its own source, callers no longer have the information needed to re-choose — the resolved value already declares where it came from, and composing it with a second source is structurally a second resolution. The rule moves the decision to exactly one place.
  • Why the detector must exist: the shape is easy to add back. A future caller looking for a fallback will reach for resolver() || fallback unless something fails loudly. The provenance-field check is that something.
Cross-reference. The arbitration class is the durable pattern behind Battle 2 — Two systems that answer the same real question will eventually disagree and Battle 7 — Wiring an existing mechanism is not the same as building new logic. The Battles hold the evidence; this class holds the name. See also R-INVARIANT-PROVENANCE and R-INVARIANT-CANONICAL.

Battles Won — Reference

This section is reference material, not required reading, and it is the Charter's historical record. The durable lesson of every battle is now a named defect class (see the preceding section) or a rule with a test. The Battles below are the evidence trail for those classes and rules — kept for traceability, not as continuous narrative. An agent reading the Charter for direction should read the rules and classes; an agent investigating a specific historical shape should read the relevant battle.

The Battles are history, and history is allowed to date. This section — and only this section — is explicitly exempt from the anti-staleness rule. T-numbers, dated decisions, and descriptions of the system at the time of a Battle are correctly historical content here. Everywhere else in the Charter, the anti-staleness rule applies in full: Rules, Principles, Governance Processes, and architectural statements must be forward-looking and must not carry mutable state.
Every battle is an instance of one recurring question:"Did I check this against the complete, real, relevant set, or just the one case that prompted the check?" The recurring shapes those instances take are catalogued in Named Defect Classes. The battles below are the historical record; TIMELINE.md is the full chronological evidence.
Battle 1A check is only as complete as the mechanisms it considers
Name every mechanism by which something could be present, and check each one. When a fix is applied at one site, sweep every sibling mechanism the same class of bug could reach. "Fixed at the reported location" is not the same as "fixed."
Evidence — T13 → T14 · T27 · T38 → T39 · T43 · T112

A bare substring check let "washer" match inside "dishwasher", silently misrouting every dishwasher complaint. The first fix — a word-boundary regex applied at all four real sites — was deployed and felt complete. Two more real gaps existed in adjacent functions the original check never considered. And in the reverse direction: a "confirmed-safe" purge list carried its own header warning "this is the single most important don't-assume item" and still turned out to be half wrong when actually checked.

TIMELINE §T13 · §T14 · §T27 · §T43 · §T112
Battle 2Two systems that answer the same real question will eventually disagree
Pick one. Retire the other. Do not "keep both to be safe." If two functions answer the same real question differently, one is eventually going to drift — and you'll find out from a client. A canonical resolver is only canonical if it is the last step. A caller-side OR, whitelist, or re-derivation on top of a canonical resolver is a second implementation, regardless of whether the resolver itself was duplicated. Extract shared logic; make other callers delegate. The recurring pattern is named DEFECT-ARBITRATION; see Named Defect Classes.
Evidence — T56 · T59 · T101 · T136

A single "Get Estimate" button fired two independent, unreconciled resolution pipelines on every click. The second listener was newer — and actively destroying the trust mechanism the client was about to interact with, overwriting a confirmation card before it was read. Separately, a real conflict-resolution mechanism existed, was correctly written, but was wired exclusively into a separate, legacy flow, never the main path that every real service actually uses. And the free-text path carried its own, second parser — so the sentence a customer watched compose could be structurally different from the sentence that got priced.

TIMELINE §T56 · §T59 · §T101 · §T136
Battle 3Derived data has a different lifecycle than its source
Compiler output is regenerable. Hand-curated business knowledge is not. Never allow a compiler to silently overwrite information it cannot itself reproduce. Preserve hand-curated content outright; use fresh derivation only as a genuine fallback. The recurring pattern is named DEFECT-DERIVED-OVERWRITE; see Named Defect Classes.
Evidence — T55 · T132

A long-standing warning said "never run this compiler and apply its output directly." That warning was inherited as received wisdom and never actually checked — until someone did, and found it was right, but for a different reason than assumed. The compiler's field names didn't match what every real consumer reads, and 12 of 38 groups had real, hand-curated content the compiler's own logic could not reproduce.

TIMELINE §T55 · §T132
Battle 4Per-visit costs do not scale with quantity
Every modifier carries an explicit scope: per-unit or per-visit. Per-visit fees are added once, outside the quantity multiplier. Per-unit fees are multiplied inside it. A universal default is not a universal truth — treat each affected service as its own real decision, not a mechanical find-and-replace. The recurring pattern is named DEFECT-PERUNIT-SCOPE; see Named Defect Classes.
Evidence — T29 · T107 · T118

A client installing ten prehung doors was quoted $295 per door — $2950 total — because a single mechanism multiplied the entire fee sum by quantity. The one-time urgency surcharge, the one-time access-difficulty fee, and the one-time parking fee were each charged ten times, once per door.

TIMELINE §T107 · §T118
Battle 5When two pieces of real evidence disagree, investigate — don't rank them
Reproduce the exact number before theorizing. Then sweep the full range, not just the reported case. Don't assume the older or less-technical evidence is wrong. And when a fix could introduce a related bug, run the full suite immediately.
Evidence — T57 · T61 · T98 → T100

A client-facing screenshot showed $1375 for a cabinet-knob install, insensitive to a real, price-affecting question. Diagnosed empirically — reproducing the exact number first, then tracing backward through the real call chain. Three distinct root-cause bugs, one of which was a downward price cliff invisible at the reported quantity but obvious across the full range.

TIMELINE §T57 · §T61 · §T98 · §T99 · §T100
Battle 6An instrument is cheaper than the bugs it finds
Before chasing symptoms, build the instrument. Honestly investigate what the test suite cannot catch — a methodology gap is not a bug in any individual test; it's a bug in the shape of the tests as a set. And when the instrument reveals its own gaps, fix them in the same session they're found.
Evidence — T105 · T106 · T107

A client reported a textarea that lost focus on every typing pause. A prior fix had debounced the re-render, which reduced how often the bug happened but never solved the actual cause. Later, a serious challenge about test-suite coverage prompted building the diagnostic layer first — and the first real trace it captured showed a severe pricing bug found from trace data alone.

TIMELINE §T105 · §T106 · §T107
Battle 7Wiring an existing mechanism is not the same as building new logic
When a fix is needed, first check whether the fix already exists. The correct mechanism may already be written and correct — and simply wired to the wrong flow. Search first, code second. The recurring pattern is named DEFECT-ARBITRATION; see Named Defect Classes.
Evidence — T101 · T70

A mutual-exclusion mechanism for conflicting tags existed, correctly written. It was wired exclusively into a separate, legacy builder flow, never the main curated-card path every real service actually uses. The fix was a single call in the right place, not new logic. Separately, a legacy dispatcher that looked like obvious debt turned out to be the feature-complete implementation of the tag-affirmation gate.

TIMELINE §T101 · §T70
Battle 8A test that passes proves only that it passed
The same correct test convention, applied twice, is not double coverage — it's the same blind spot twice. When a test passes, ask which failure modes it cannot catch. A deliberately different kind of test is what closes the gap, not a third repeat of the same one.
Evidence — T42 · T50

A prototype's dropdown worked but nothing rendered afterward, with no visible error. Both existing tests passed throughout — because each correctly set both required globals in its own sandbox, testing the engine logic but never the actual page's own bootstrapping. Separately, a systemic duplication was found and extracted into a shared function — obviously correct — and broke 15 existing test files across three attempts.

TIMELINE §T42 · §T50
Battle 9A field that is never set is not the same as a field correctly defaulted
Trace what the default means downstream. When two bugs cancel each other into a broken-but-not-obviously-broken outcome, fixing one exposes the other — and that exposure is a feature, not a regression.
Evidence — T30

A third entry point never set the intent field at all — so a downstream function always silently defaulted to "Repair" for every entry through that path. A complete sweep confirmed this affected 20 real groups whose real types are never "Repair." Verifying the fix across all 20 surfaced a second, independent, pre-existing data bug.

TIMELINE §T30
Battle 10Fix a collision at the data level when you can
Fix it in shared matching logic only when you must. The narrower fix has a narrower blast radius. A conditional rule stated once in prose is not sufficient instruction for delegation — the per-row check needs its own explicit verification step.
Evidence — T78 · T32

A smart tag's synonym collided with another tag's synonym — the same class of bug as a word-boundary collision fixed twice before. The fix was a two-word phrase, not a change to shared matching logic. Separately, a delegated audit correctly found every tag with an override and correctly mapped tags to services — but never applied its own, correctly-stated conditional rule per row.

TIMELINE §T78 · §T32
Battle 11A correct conclusion can be quietly invalidated by later work
Keep re-verifying against current reality. Do not treat a past "confirmed safe" as permanent. The only way to catch a silent invalidation is to keep asking whether the same mechanism is still doing what the earlier conclusion assumed it was.
Evidence — T83

A historical backlog's own "confirmed safe" conclusion was re-checked against current code — and found to have been quietly invalidated by a later, unrelated session's work touching the same mechanism from a different angle. A real, live modifier fee that a service's own inherent tag should always have applied, never did.

TIMELINE §T83
Battle 12The guardrail is what turns "this looks done" into "this is verifiably done"
Before any strangler-fig cutover, build the guardrail the plan asks for — and let it do its job. Skipping ahead to the cutover ships real, newly-discovered regression risk to the primary entry point.
Evidence — T68 → T69 → T70

A legacy function looked like obvious debt and was slated for removal. The first "safety check" caught a real gap that would have shipped as a live regression if the retirement had proceeded. The second investigation found the actual root cause was different from the first hypothesis, and fixing it introduced a real regression caught by the test suite doing its job.

TIMELINE §T68 · §T69 · §T70
Battle 13A debounce is not a fix
Interaction frequency is not the same as correctness. Debouncing, throttling, or reducing render frequency can hide a symptom without fixing the state-management or DOM-lifecycle defect underneath it. When responsiveness work is needed, measure the real bottleneck and verify behavior against the actual DOM and preserved state.
When a bug has a mechanism, look at the mechanism, not at the frequency. Save and restore real state around the render, and verify it with a real DOM — a code-shape check will miss the class of bug that only shows up when the DOM is genuinely destroyed and recreated.
Evidence — T105

A prior session's own fix had debounced a re-render to reduce how often focus was lost. It never solved the actual cause: the render destroys and recreates the focused element on every pause, and destroying a focused element necessarily drops focus.

TIMELINE §T105
Battle 14Some questions are removed, not reframed
When a question has a real pricing consequence but the fact can be captured structurally, remove the question — do not reframe it. Reframing is for questions the client must answer but can answer more easily. Removal is for questions the system should never ask at all.
Evidence — T135

An operator override established that parking and urgency must never be asked as intake questions, despite both carrying real per-visit fees. The facts are captured structurally — parking from the service context, the navigation path, or free-text inference; urgency from the client's own words or the service itself. The fees still apply when the facts are known. The questions do not exist.

The second lesson: the Ad-Lib sentence names the answer that a chip produced, not the chip itself. A chip is a control; an answer is a fact. Composing chip clauses into the sentence conflates the two, and the trust mechanism the sentence exists to provide is weaker for it.

TIMELINE §T135
Battle 15The preview must be the price
One parse, one composer, one canonical intent. When the customer watches one sentence compose and a different one gets priced, the trust mechanism the whole system is built on is broken. The single-parse rule is a structural test, not a policy. The recurring pattern is named DEFECT-DUPLICATE-PARSER; see Named Defect Classes.
Evidence — T136

Four operator screenshots and a trace export showed the preview and the route disagreeing on the same request. Root cause confirmed in code and trace: the file carried four independently-evolved parsers (the canonical NLP block plus a private nested copy inside the live preview), and three module-level functions were declared twice with the later copy silently shadowing the earlier. One pair held two different real fixes that had never been live together. The fix was structural: one parse, one composer, one context, enforced by a test that fails if the boundary is violated.

TIMELINE §T136
Battle 16A renderer may only clear what it owns
Every render target is a leaf host. A renderer clears only its own host. A host is never nested inside another concern’s host. When two concerns render, their hosts are disjoint. A test must check visibility, not presence: a node under a display:none ancestor is present in the DOM and invisible to the customer.
Evidence — T152

In a real browser, the original qr.html responded to 2 of 8 ordinary requests. The cause was architectural: #serviceRequestSummary was both the cart panel and the render target for affirmation and self-quote cards, and it was nested inside #intakeQuestionsContainer. renderRoute cleared intakeQuestionsContainer.innerHTML = '', destroying the cart panel. Every card renderer then did const c = document.getElementById('serviceRequestSummary'); if (!c) return; and returned silently. The cart’s cached DOM references went stale. The fix was to make the hosts flat siblings and to make renderRoute clear only what it owns. None of this was visible to the differential test because both old and new were dead; the test that caught it was a strict-visibility journey test that walked eight ordinary requests in a real browser.

TIMELINE §T152
Battle 17A gate that cannot tell formatting from behavior
A change-based gate must compare semantics, not text. When a change’s diff is dominated by formatting, the gate either normalizes formatting before comparison or the formatting change is isolated into its own commit so semantic changes are gated on their merits. A gate that goes red on a wholesale reformat for functions the reformat did not touch semantically is failing for the wrong reason.
Evidence — T156

The operator reformatted qr.html wholesale. The ship gate compares base and head and fails any new, changed, or moved function that does not comply with its layer. The reformat made 185 of 198 functions “changed,” and the gate went red on four functions that had always violated their layer. The gate was correct in its rule but wrong in its measurement: it could not distinguish a semantic change from formatting noise. The fix was not to lower the gate but to make it a semantic comparison (or to isolate the reformat).

TIMELINE §T156
Battle 18The cart is not presentation; it is a ledger
A cart line’s identity includes every fact its price depends on. Two requests that price differently are never merged. A line’s total is its amount times its quantity, computed in one place. A stored quantity that no total reads is not a quantity; it is a number on a screen.
Evidence — T158

Two identical taps were quoted once: the merge raised qty, but the summary, the overlay, and both totals summed the price strings and never read qty. Three shelves ($478) and one shelf ($159), same service, same answers, merged into one $478 line; the $159 request vanished. The cart’s identity omitted the quoted amount, which the price depends on. The fix was to include the quoted amount in the key, record the quantity the card priced, and compute the total as amount × quantity in one place. The defect was found by driving the real cart in a browser; the unit tests did not see it because they tested the merge function, not the total.

TIMELINE §T158
Battle 19No way out
Every question that constrains an answer offers an escape — “Other”, “Don’t know”, or “None of these” — that routes to a priced path. Every question declares its purpose so the UI can present it honestly. A question with no escape is a dead end; a question with no purpose is opaque.

Historical clarification: the original fix added escape choices and purpose fields; that evidence records the first repair, not proof that every escape already diverged into a distinct downstream state. The current Rule is sharper: an escape must change the downstream state, including by routing to a named service, rather than merely repeat the confident-answer path.

Evidence — T169

The brand question offered three brands and no “Other / don’t know”; a customer with a brand not listed had no answer. Eight modules had no purpose field, so the UI could not decide whether to show an “affects price” badge. Both are the same failure: a question that does not tell the customer what it is for and does not let them say “I don’t know” is a question that does not belong. The fix added “Other / don’t know” to the brand question and purpose to the eight modules. The detector that now holds it reads the catalog, not a list of names.

TIMELINE §T169
ClosingThe Deepest Lesson
There is one question that has found nearly every genuinely severe bug in this entire project:"Did I check this against the complete, real, relevant set, or just the one case that prompted the check?"
Every time a finding or a fix looked complete, a deliberate, complete sweep — not just re-checking the original reported case — found it wasn't, or found there was less real work left than assumed in the opposite direction. This is not a flaw in the process; it is the process working.
The full curriculum — TIMELINE.md

Every battle above is a specific, traceable instance of this one question. The complete chronological record lives in TIMELINE.md, and the auto-generated cross-reference lives in AGGREGATOR.html.

TIMELINE.md — full curriculum · AGGREGATOR.html — cross-reference

Glossary

Every glossary entry has a stable anchor and appears in the searchable reference index. A dagger marker links to the glossary where the body explicitly marks a term; the index remains the complete term directory, including terms that are not marked inline.

TermDefinition
Chip†A smart-tag chip: a #smart_tag shown beneath the text input, generated as the client types. A chip labels and may prompt; it is never a pricing input (P-INVARIANT-CHIPSNOTFEES).
Answer / response†What the client selects or states in the guided builder or in a question. Answers carry facts and time; through time they carry price. They are not called chips.
Cart line†One priced request held in the cart. Its identity is every fact its price depends on (R-PRICE-LINEIDENTITY); its total is its amount times its quantity.
Render host†The DOM element a renderer clears and fills. Every host is a leaf; hosts are never nested one inside another and no two renderers share a host (R-SYSTEM-HOSTS).
Escape answer†An explicit “Other”, “Don’t know”, or “None of these” option on a constrained question. It routes to a priced path — a conditional quote, a diagnostic, or a route_to pointing at a named service — that differs from the confident answers’ downstream state (R-INTAKE-ESCAPE). It is not a “not sure, the tech will measure” fallback (R-INTAKE-NONRETIRING).
Question purpose†One of pricing, diagnostic, or routing — the question’s declared intent, not the Wire-or-Remove effect category. The actual effect must separately be Price, Time, Branch, or Prep. A question with no purpose, or with the purpose informational, is prohibited by P-INTAKE-QTEST and R-INTAKE-WIREREMOVE.
BookingContextThe canonical convergence contract containing the resolved facts needed by downstream pricing, presentation, and booking. All supported entry modes converge into this context.
ResolvedRouteThe orchestrator's resolved execution result. It is related to, but not an alias for, BookingContext; the exact object shape belongs to the owning implementation specification.
EntityThe conceptual thing in the world being discussed, such as a Door Lever, Window, or Toilet. Product-specific details are attributes.
Behavioral ClassThe domain-model class formerly called an archetype: a reusable behavioral classification that tells the system how an entity is generally understood and handled.
Routing ModeThe orthogonal concern describing how confidence and scope are built. It is exposed in the derived runtime artifact as routing_archetypes; the key name is implementation, not the conceptual term.
Component / Symptom / ActionThe three real axes a picker can descend through. Component and Symptom are shapes — the group's routing mode determines which one leads, because that is what the customer arrives with for this family of work. Action is a secondary refinement under the component or symptom, asked only where the group genuinely has more than one real action.
Pricing ModeThe orthogonal concern describing how cost is calculated. The live implementation field names remain owned by the SSOT/schema; the aggregate pricing_archetypes namespace is documentation, not runtime.
CapabilityA reusable statement of what can meaningfully happen to or with an entity. An execution concept, not a mandatory authored identity layer.
ActionThe concrete operation instantiated for the current job.
StrategyThe method by which the system gathers or infers evidence needed to resolve work. An execution concept, not a mandatory authored identity layer.
Derived ServiceA customer-facing expression of resolved scope; it is derived from the domain model rather than serving as its foundation.
Dynamic ServiceA priceable resolution composed at runtime from the entity/action/modifier space, not authored as its own named service entry. Keyed by its resolved scope: the entity, the action, the applicable modifiers, and the group the request resolved through.
merge_strategyA dynamic service's declared relationship to its owning group's defaults. It is a resolution-time rule, not a second source of identity; the composed service and the group remain one authored system.
Authored sourceThe entity-first domain knowledge edited by humans through the governed authoring surface. Contains curated business truth: entities, questions, options, uncertainty classes, confidence policies, and picker vocabulary. Not fetched at runtime. Referenced in this Charter as the authoritative authored-data source.
Derived runtime artifactThe machine-optimized graph generated from the authored source by the compiler. Consumed by the runtime. Carries a provenance reference to the source so every value it contains can be traced back to the business knowledge that authored it. Referenced in this Charter as the derived runtime artifact.
SSOTThe single authored source of business knowledge, edited through the governed authoring surface. The derived runtime artifact is a published projection of that source, not a second source of truth; it carries provenance back to the authored source. Together they form one authored authority and one derived projection, with a traceable chain between them.
CᵢIntent confidence, normalized to 0–1. It expresses how strongly the system understands what the client wants.
CₓScope confidence, normalized to 0–1. It expresses how strongly the system knows the physical/work conditions required for the active pricing path.
τₓThe required scope-confidence threshold for the active resolution/pricing policy. It is an explicit policy input, not a universal scalar-complexity formula.
Uncertainty classA condition whose answer could materially affect price, labor, routing, scheduling, or preparation. Confidence is measured as the proportion of relevant uncertainty classes retired — not as a probability of correctness, and not as a count of questions answered.
Uncertainty ledgerThe live set of uncertainty classes relevant to the current resolved job and pricing path, including both retired and open classes. Its membership changes when job shape changes; retired classes remain represented while still relevant so the confidence denominator does not shrink merely because evidence was obtained.
Evidence eventAn accepted fact or evidence contribution that updates BookingContext. Evidence events may come from navigation, NLP, catalog selection, chips, guided intake, photographs, documents, model numbers, or other supported mechanisms. They are inputs to the canonical resolution model, not separate confidence models.
Material mutationA canonical BookingContext change that can alter resolved scope, complexity, uncertainty, confidence, threshold policy, precision, pricing, scheduling, routing, or checkout state. Material mutations require downstream re-resolution before the next decision under R-RESOLUTION-RECOMPUTE.
FrictionThe Charter uses max(0, τₓ − Cₓ) as the remaining scope-confidence gap metric, historically displayed as Friction. This metric is not itself a unit of customer effort. Customer effort is the economic cost of acquiring evidence and is evaluated directionally by the friction-efficiency principle; the Charter does not invent a universal effort scalar.
PrecisionThe specificity of the priced outcome presented to the client: exact quote, bounded range, conditional quote, or registered diagnostic path.
Checkout StateThe presentation state that communicates how the resolved price is justified. It does not calculate the price.
Smart tagsSSOT data used by the NLP/input path to represent recognized concepts and facts. They are not pricing factors themselves.
Fact scopingThe three narrowing levels — category, group, service exclusion — at which a smart tag's applicability is declared. The exclusion layer exists because a real group can legitimately contain both the fact-applying and the fact-not-applying case.
intent_mappings.objectsThe distinct catalog-search mapping system. It overlaps with smart tags but is not the same tag set.
full_weight_synonymsA per-entry list of synonyms that are as specific and unambiguous as the entry's own keyword. A listed synonym scores at the entry's full confidence_weight; an unlisted synonym scores at half weight. Promotion is earned by specificity, not by usefulness.
High-density evidenceEvidence that can reliably establish multiple independent facts relevant to routing, scope, pricing, scheduling, or preparation under the active service rules — a photograph, a model number, a manufacturer label. The platform prefers high-density evidence over equivalent low-density questionnaires when service rules permit.
Foundational artifactAn artifact other work is built on top of — the SSOT's graph, a schema's core premise, an authored contract. A change to a foundational artifact's shape is governed differently from a local shape change; see G-GOVERN-TRANSITION and G-GOVERN-RAISE.
Resolution provenanceThe named source of a resolved value, carried on the resolver's own return value (source: 'service_override' | 'archetype_default' | 'dynamic_engine' | 'fallback' | 'answer_override'; the last names a customer answer that superseded the resolved value). A resolved value that cannot be traced to such a source on its own return value is a defect under R-INVARIANT-PROVENANCE.
Named defect classA recurring bug pattern — as distinct from a single bug instance — catalogued in Named Defect Classes with its signature, owning invariants, and detector. The first entry is DEFECT-ARBITRATION. Naming a class is what makes G-INVARIANT-DETECTOR actionable.
Rule codeA stable, flat identifier of the form R-<DOMAIN>-<NAME> attached to every citable Rule. Codes survive amendments; rule text may change. Used for navigation, conversation, verification evidence, and governance amendments. The code does not encode coverage or enforcement status.
Amendment logThe collapsed <details> block under an amended rule. Preserves the original text struck through, the new text, the date, and an "Operator Override — Authorized" tag. The top of the page always shows the current rule; the history is one click below.
T-numberA historical entry reference in the historical record, used by battle evidence trails.

Document Scope

The Charter is the durable North Star: vision, principles, architectural boundaries, business invariants, and the lessons that should survive implementation changes.

It is not the field-level schema, not a tutorial, and not a task roadmap. Authored business knowledge lives in the authoritative authored-data source (edited through the governed authoring surface); the runtime consumes the derived the derived runtime artifact, which carries a provenance reference back to the source. Current code state, contracts, ownership, open decisions, and historical evidence live in the engineering documents named in the Document Relationship Map above. When those sources disagree with this Charter, they take precedence — and the disagreement itself is a tracked engineering issue, not something resolved by silently editing one document to make the conflict disappear.

Historical exemption. The Battles Won reference and its evidence trails in Part XI are the only material explicitly exempt from the anti-staleness rule. The exemption applies to the historical battle records, not to the whole Part XI index, glossary, or other material. Everything else in the Charter is forward-looking and durable.

Document History

The authoritative revision history, dated decisions, and task-level evidence live in the historical record. This Project Charter does not duplicate that ledger.

Where everything else lives.

This Project Charter states the durable principles. Authored business knowledge lives in the authoritative authored-data source and is edited through the governed authoring surface. the derived runtime artifact is the derived runtime artifact the engine consumes; it carries a provenance reference to the source. Field contracts live in the authoritative schema; module ownership lives in the maintained component-responsibility map. Historical evidence and open decisions live in the historical record and the decision ledger; the evidence/status projection provides the cross-reference/status projection. Precedence: executable reality establishes what is live; the owning specification establishes the intended contract; evidence establishes what happened; the Charter establishes durable direction.
